# PART 2 — RUNBOOK.md

# Horowitz Agent — Orchestrator Runbook

Purpose: build a chat agent that answers as if it were Ben Horowitz, grounded strictly in his public writing, talks, interviews, and podcasts. This document is written for an orchestrator agent (frontier model, e.g. Claude Opus/Fable in Claude Code or Hermes) that plans, delegates bulk work to cheaper models, verifies, and ships.

## 0. Operating rules for the orchestrator

- You own the plan, the acceptance checks, and every irreversible action. Cheap models own volume: scraping, transcription cleanup, chunk labeling, first-draft summaries.
- Delegation pattern for every bulk task: write a task spec with input, output schema, and a validation script; fan out to a cheap model (Haiku / open-weight); run the validator; sample 5% by hand (i.e., read them yourself); only then mark done.
- Everything lives in one Git repo: $PROJECT_ROOT/horowitz-agent. Commit at the end of every phase with a decisions/NNN-*.md record explaining choices made.
- Never fabricate a source. A source that cannot be fetched and verified is not in the bibliography.
- Legal constraints (non-negotiable):
  - Ingest only content that is publicly accessible (blog posts, published transcripts, podcasts, videos) or that DJ has personally purchased (books) for private use.
  - The bot and corpus are private, single-user tools. Never publish the corpus, book text, or the bot.
  - Label the agent “unofficial; synthesized from Ben Horowitz’s public statements” in its system prompt and UI.
- Spend limits: set a hard API budget per phase before starting it (defaults below). Stop and report if 80% is consumed before the phase is done.
- If a step is blocked (paywall, rate limit, missing credential), log it in BLOCKERS.md and continue with what is available. Do not stall the whole pipeline.

### Human gates (hard stops)
- GATE 1 — after Phase A. Do not start Phase B until DJ has reviewed bibliography.md and replied “approved” (optionally with exclusions/additions). Produce the Gate 1 packet described in §2.5, then end your turn. If running unattended, exit with status AWAITING_GATE_1.
- GATE 2 — after Phase C. Present style_guide.md, belief_map.md, and 5 sample answers before deploying the runtime. Stop until approved.
- GATE 3 — before any cost above $50 in a single phase. Stop and confirm.

### Verification rules

- Every acceptance check must be evidence, not assertion: the run log records the validator command, its exit code, and the sampled file IDs you read. “I checked and it looks good” without a listed sample is a failed check.
- Any judgment-based check (quality of cleaning, persona fidelity) is performed by a separate model call with no memory of the work — not by the model that produced it.
- Keep RUNLOG.md: one entry per task with timestamp, model used, tokens/cost, validator result, and sample IDs reviewed. DJ audits this at each gate.

## 1. Repo layout
horowitz-agent/
  README.md
  BLOCKERS.md
  decisions/            # one ADR per major choice
  bibliography/
    bibliography.jsonl  # one record per source (schema §2.3)
    bibliography.md     # human-readable, grouped by medium and year
  raw/                  # untouched fetched artifacts (html, audio, pdf)
  corpus/               # cleaned markdown, one file per source
  chunks/               # chunks.jsonl (schema §3.2)
  persona/
    style_guide.md
    belief_map.md
    system_prompt.md
  agent/                # runtime code
  eval/
    questions.jsonl
    results/
  scripts/              # ingestion, chunking, embedding, validators

Stack defaults (override only with an ADR): Python 3.11, uv, Postgres + pgvector on Railway, yt-dlp + faster-whisper for audio, Anthropic API for orchestration and the runtime age

Confirm the branch is already in sync with GitHub.\n2. Poll the live app for a few minutes and compare a stable marker (HTML build ID, script chunk hashes, or another response detail that should change on deploy).\n4. If the push lands on GitHub but the live app stays identical, the Railway GitHub connection is not active.\n\n**Connection clues:**\n- In Railway settings, a connected service shows a repo/branch; a \"Connect Repo\" prompt means it is not wired.\n- If the service env exposes repo metadata and the owner field is empty, treat that as strong evidence the GitHub connection is missing.\n\n**Always check repo sync before connecting:**\n``​`bash\n# Check local repo status vs remote\ncd /path/to/project && git status && git log --oneline -5\n# Shows if local is ahead of origin — means GitHub may be stale\n``​`\n\n**If push is rejected, rebase first:**\n- Fetch the remote commit with token auth.\n- Rebase local `main` onto the fetched remote branch.\n- Push again.\n- See `references/github-push-rebase-fallback.md` for the exact sequence.\n\n**Setup flow when not connected:**\n1. It covers two paths:\n\n- **`git` (always available)** — uses HTTPS personal access tokens or SSH keys\n- **`gh` CLI (if installed)** — richer GitHub API access with a simpler auth flow\n\n## Detection Flow\n\nWhen a user asks you to work with GitHub, run this check first:\n\n``​`bash\n# Check what's available\ngit --version\ngh --version 2>/dev/null || echo \"gh not installed\"\n\n# Check if already authenticated\ngh auth status 2>/dev/null || echo \"gh not authenticated\"\ngit config --global credential.helper 2>/dev/null || echo \"no git credential helper\"\n``​`\n\n**Decision tree:**\n1. If `gh auth status` shows authenticated → you're good, use `gh` for everything\n2. If `gh` is installed but not authenticated → use \"gh auth\" method below\n3. In DJ's Hermes/Railway environment, stale `GITHUB_TOKEN` values can coexist with valid OAuth/PAT tokens in durable state. `git ls-remote` can be misleading if public read access succeeds or cached credentials are involved; validate authenticated access with the GitHub `/user` API and/or a write-permission operation without printing secrets. Search likely stores and validate candidates with the GitHub `/user` API without printing secrets:\n\n``​`bash\npython3 - <<'PY'\nfrom pathlib import Path\nimport re, urllib.request, json\npatterns = [r'ghp_[A-Za-z0-9_]{36}', r'github_pat_[A-Za-z0-9_]+', r'gho_[A-Za-z0-9_]{36}', r'ghs_[A-Za-z0-9_]{36}']\nseen = []\nfor root in [Path('/opt/data'), Path.home()]:\n    if not root.exists():\n        continue\n    for p in root.rglob('*'):\n        if not p.is_file():\n            continue\n        try:\n            if p.stat().st_size > 5_000_000:\n                continue\n            text = p.read_text(errors='ignore')\n        except Exception:\n            continue\n        for pat in patterns:\n            for m in re.finditer(pat, text):\n                tok = m.group(0)\n                if tok not in seen:\n                    seen.append(tok)\nfor i, tok in enumerate(seen, 1):\n    req = urllib.request.Request('https://api.github.com/user', headers={'Authorization': f'Bearer {tok}', 'Accept': 'application/vnd.github+json', 'User-Agent': 'hermes'})\n    try:\n        with urllib.request.urlopen(req, timeout=10) as r:\n            data = json.load(r)\n        print('VALID_CANDIDATE', i, data.get('login'), tok[:8], tok[-4:])\n    except Exception:\n        pass\nPY\n``​`\n\nUse the valid candidate through `GIT_ASKPASS` or `gh auth login --with-token`; do not paste tokens into output or leave them embedded in remotes.\n\n---\n\n## Method 1: Git-Only Authentication (No gh, No sudo)\n\nThis works on any machine with `git` installed. tr,en\n``​`\n\nIf the active Python environment has no `pip` (common in stripped Hermes installs), install the dependency with uv instead:\n\n``​`bash\nuv pip install --python /opt/hermes/.venv/bin/python youtube-transcript-api\n``​`\n\n## Output Formats\n\nAfter fetching the transcript, format it based on what the user asks for:\n\n- **Chapters**: Group by topic shifts, output timestamped chapter list\n- **Summary**: Concise 5-10 sentence overview of the entire video\n- **Chapter summaries**: Chapters with a short paragraph summary for each\n- **Thread**: Twitter/X thread format — numbered posts, each under 280 chars\n- **Blog post**: Full article with title, sections, and key takeaways\n- **Quotes**: Notable quotes with timestamps\n\n### Example — Chapters Output\n\n``​`\n00:00 Introduction — host opens with the problem statement\n03:45 Background — prior work and why existing solutions fall short\n12:20 Core method — walkthrough of the proposed approach\n24:10 Results — benchmark comparisons and key takeaways\n31:55 Q&A — audience questions on scalability and next steps\n``​`\n\n## Workflow\n\n1. **Identify sources**: if the user asks for “new/recent” videos without URLs, web-search for the likely title/guest/show, prefer official YouTube/podcast pages, and capture title, show, date, URL, and video ID.\n2. **Fetch** the transcript Do not summarize when the user asked for raw transcripts unless they also request analysis.\n6. **Transform** into the requested output format. Deliver the artifact with `MEDIA:/absolute/path`.\n\n## Error Handling\n\n- **Transcript disabled**: tell the user; suggest they check if subtitles are available on the video page.\n- **Private/unavailable video**: relay the error and ask the user to verify the URL.\n- **No matching language**: retry without `--language` to fetch any available transcript, then note the actual language to the user.\n- **Dependency missing**: run `pip install youtube-transcript-api` and retry.\n", "path": "media/youtube-content/SKILL.md", "skill_dir": "/opt/data/skills/media/youtube-content", "linked_files": {"references": ["references/output-formats.md"], "scripts": ["scripts/fetch_transcript.py"]}, "usage_hint": "To view linked files, call skill_view(name, file_path) where file_path is e.g. 'references/api.md' or 'assets/config.yaml'", "required_environment_variables": [], "required_commands": [], "missing_required_environment_variables": [], "missing_credential_files": [], "missing_required_commands": [], "setup_needed": false, "setup_skipped": false, "readiness_status": "available"}

{"path":"/opt/data/projects/horowitz-agent/RUNBOOK.md","content":"# Horowitz Agent — Orchestrator Runbook\n\nPurpose: build a chat agent that answers as if it were Ben Horowitz, grounded strictly in his public writing, talks, interviews, and podcasts. This document is written for an orchestrator agent (frontier model, e.g. Claude Opus/Fable in Claude Code or Hermes) that plans, delegates bulk work to cheaper models, verifies, and ships.\n\n## 0. Operating rules for the orchestrator\n\n- You own the plan, the acceptance checks, and every irreversible action. Cheap models own volume: scraping, transcription cleanup, chunk labeling, first-draft summaries.\n- Delegation pattern for every bulk task: write a task spec with input, output schema, and a validation script; fan out to a cheap model (Haiku / open-weight); run the validator; sample 5% by hand (i.e., read them yourself); only then mark done.\n- Everything lives in one Git repo: $PROJECT_ROOT/horowitz-agent. Commit at the end of every phase with a decisions/NNN-*.md record explaining choices made.\n- Never fabricate a source. A source that cannot be fetched and verified is not in the bibliography.\n- Legal constraints (non-negotiable):\n  - Ingest only content that is publicly accessible (blog posts, published transcripts, podcasts, videos) or that DJ has personally purchased (books) for private use.\n  - The bot and corpus are private, single-user tools. Never publish the corpus, book text, or the bot.\n  - Label the agent “unofficial; synthesized from Ben Horowitz’s public statements” in its system prompt and UI.\n- Spend limits: set a hard API budget per phase before starting it (defaults below). Stop and report if 80% is consumed before the phase is done.\n- If a step is blocked (paywall, rate limit, missing credential), log it in BLOCKERS.md and continue with what is available. Do not stall the whole pipeline.\n\n### Human gates (hard stops)\n- GATE 1 — after Phase A. Do not start Phase B until DJ has reviewed bibliography.md and replied “approved” (optionally with exclusions/additions). Produce the Gate 1 packet described in §2.5, then end your turn. If running unattended, exit with status AWAITING_GATE_1.\n- GATE 2 — after Phase C. Present style_guide.md, belief_map.md, and 5 sample answers before deploying the runtime. Stop until approved.\n- GATE 3 — before any cost above $50 in a single phase. Stop and confirm.\n\n### Verification rules\n\n- Every acceptance check must be evidence, not assertion: the run log records the validator command, its exit code, and the sampled file IDs you read. “I checked and it looks good” without a listed sample is a failed check.\n- Any judgment-based check (quality of cleaning, persona fidelity) is performed by a separate model call with no memory of the work — not by the model that produced it.\n- Keep RUNLOG.md: one entry per task with timestamp, model used, tokens/cost, validator result, and sample IDs reviewed. DJ audits this at each gate.\n\n## 1. Repo layout\nhorowitz-agent/\n  README.md\n  BLOCKERS.md\n  decisions/            # one ADR per major choice\n  bibliography/\n    bibliography.jsonl  # one record per source (schema §2.3)\n    bibliography.md     # human-readable, grouped by medium and year\n  raw/                  # untouched fetched artifacts (html, audio, pdf)\n  corpus/               # cleaned markdown, one file per source\n  chunks/               # chunks.jsonl (schema §3.2)\n  persona/\n    style_guide.md\n    belief_map.m

(Our pod The Stories & Code of Culture Change discusses Ben’s most recent book.)\n    23|\n    24|During the conversation, Reed tells the story of Netflix’s evolution and his management philosophy, including the hard lesson he learned about what happens when you optimize for efficiency at the expense of creative talent. He also explains why sometimes a more narrow market focus is better for growth and shares the tactics that have helped Netflix expand globally and translate a culture of innovation across different countries, from Japan to Brazil to America.\n    25|\n    26|## Transcript\n    27|\n    28|Ben: One of the reasons I really liked your book is you start from that honest place of “I screwed it up and now I need to figure it out.” You did that at your prior company Pure Software, which needed to reinvent itself, but couldn’t. What were the things that you ran into at Pure that kept you from being able to reinvent?\n    29|\n    30|Reed: I was a first-time manager. If you find an error in software, you try to build a regression suite or test for it that runs to make sure that error doesn’t come back, and that’s good design and good engineering.\n    31|\n    32|I viewed the organization as a big software puzzle, which I know is laughably simplistic, but that’s how I viewed it. And so, every time there was an error, we’d put a process in place to make sure that error didn’t happen. And, indeed, generally that particular error didn’t happen. continue\n        if re.fullmatch(r'(##\\s*)?Ben[’\\']?s Blog', s, re.I): continue\n        if re.fullmatch(r'(##\\s*)?a16z\\.com', s, re.I): continue\n        if re.fullmatch(r'(##\\s*)?(Tweet|X|Linkedin|Facebook|Copy link)', s, re.I): continue\n        if 'Click here to preorder' in s: continue\n        if 'available on Amazon' in s and len(s)<180: continue\n        lines.append(line)\n    body='\\n'.join(lines)\n    # remove comments/replies remnants\n    body=re.sub(r'\\n## \\d+ Replies\\b.*','',body,flags=re.I|re.S)\n    # de-duplicate repeated paragraph blocks while preserving order\n    paras=[p.strip() for p in re.split(r'\\n\\s*\\n', body) if p.strip()]\n    out=[]; seen=set()\n    for p in paras:\n        key=re.sub(r'\\W+',' ',p.lower()).strip()\n        if len(key)>30 and key in seen: continue\n        seen.add(key); out.append(p)\n    body='\\n\\n'.join(out).strip()+'\\n'\n    # for podcast transcript, cut after obvious non-transcript link-card tail: last speaker-labeled paragraph if followed by non-speaker junk\n    if medium=='podcast':\n        paras=body.split('\\n\\n')\n        last=-1\n        for i,p in enumerate(paras):\n            if re.match(r'^(Ben|Reed|Marc|Host|Speaker \\d+|[A-Z][a-z]+):', p.strip()): last=i\n        if last>=0 and len(paras)-last>8:\n            body='\\n\\n'.join(paras[:last+1]).strip()+'\\n'\n    return body\nrecords=[json.loads(l) for l in (BIB/'bibliography.jsonl').read_text().splitlines() if l.strip()]\nchanged=0\nfor r in records:\n    p=CORPUS/(r['id']+'.md')\n    if r['status']!='ingested' or not p.exists(): continue\n    txt=p.read_text(encoding='utf-8'); parts=txt.split('---\\n',2); body=parts[2] if len(parts)>2 else txt\n    body2=clean_body(body,r['medium'])\n    # New Yorker seed: restrict to paragraphs mentioning/quoting Ben; mark mixed context\n    if 'newyorker.com' in r.get('url',''):\n        paras=[p for p in body2.split('\\n\\n') if re.search(r'\\b(Ben|Horowitz)\\b',p)]\n        body2='\\n\\n'.join(paras).strip()+'\\n'\n        r['notes']='Cleaned to paragraphs mentioning Ben/Horowitz only; third-party context remains for quotes.'\n    r['word_count']=wc(body2)\n    fm={k:r.get(k,'') for k in ['id','title','medium','date','url','publisher_or_show','role','status','source_hash','word_count','transcribed','notes']}\n    p.write_text('---\\n'+yaml.safe_dump(fm,sort_keys=False,allow_unicode=True)+'---\\n\\n'+body2,encoding='utf-8'); changed+=1\n(BIB/'bibliography.jsonl').write_text('\\n'.join(json.dumps(r,ensure_ascii=False) for r in records)+'\\n',encoding='utf-8')\n# regenerate bib md, sample pa

{"content":"#!/usr/bin/env python3\nimport json,re,yaml\nfrom pathlib import Path\nfrom bs4 import BeautifulSoup\nROOT=Path(__file__).resolve().parents[1]\nrecs=[json.loads(l) for l in open(ROOT/'bibliography/bibliography.jsonl') if l.strip()]\ndef wc(t): return len(re.findall(r\"\\b[\\w'’.-]+\\b\",t or ''))\ndef extract_entry(raw):\n    soup=BeautifulSoup(raw,'lxml')\n    el=soup.select_one('div.entry-content') or soup.select_one('article') or soup\n    for tag in el(['script','style','object','embed','iframe','form']): tag.decompose()\n    for bad in el.select('.sharedaddy,.sd-sharing-enabled,.postmeta,.navigation,.comments,.commentlist,#comments'):\n        bad.decompose()\n    lines=[]\n    for node in el.find_all(['h1','h2','h3','p','li','blockquote']):\n        tx=node.get_text(' ',strip=True)\n        if not tx: continue\n        if re.fullmatch(r'(Tweet|Digg|Facebook|Email|Print|Share)',tx,re.I): continue\n        if re.search(r'^(Artist:|Lyrics$|Label:)',tx): continue\n        lines.append(('## '+tx) if node.name in ['h1','h2','h3'] else tx)\n    paras=[]; seen=set()\n    for p in lines:\n        key=re.sub(r'\\W+',' ',p.lower()).strip()\n        if len(key)>30 and key in seen: continue\n        seen.add(key); paras.append(p)\n    text='\\n\\n'.join(paras)\n    text=re.sub(r'\\n## \\d+ Replies\\b.*','',text,flags=re.I|re.S)\n    return text.strip()+'\\n'\nfor r in recs:\n    if r['status']=='ingested' and r['medium']=='blog':\n        rawp=ROOT/'raw/html'/(r['id']+'.html'); cp=ROOT/'corpus'/(r['id']+'.md')\n        if rawp.exists() and cp.exists():\n            body=extract_entry(rawp.read_text(errors='ignore'))\n            if wc(body)>wc(cp.read_text(errors='ignore'))*0.8 or r['word_count']<100:\n                r['word_count']=wc(body)\n                fm={k:r.get(k,'') for k in ['id','title','medium','date','url','publisher_or_show','role','status','source_hash','word_count','transcribed','notes']}\n                cp.write_text('---\\n'+yaml.safe_dump(fm,sort_keys=False,allow_unicode=True)+'---\\n\\n'+body)\n# fix podcast tail after transcript: remove any final non-speaker recommendation block beginning at standalone titles after transcript if no colon\nfor r in recs:\n    if r['id']=='[REDACTED_PHONE]-podcast-a16z-podcast-designing-a-culture-of-reinvention':\n        cp=ROOT/'corpus'/(r['id']+'.md'); r['word_count']=wc(body)\n        fm={k:r.get(k,'') for k in ['id','title','medium','date','url','publisher_or_show','role','status','source_hash','word_count','transcribed','notes']}\n        cp.write_text('---\\n'+yaml.safe_dump(fm,sort_keys=False,allow_unicode=True)+'---\\n\\n'+body)\nopen(ROOT/'bibliography/bibliography.jsonl','w').write('\\n'.join(json.dumps(r,ensure_ascii=False) for r in recs)+'\\n')\nprint('fixed')\n","path":"/opt/data/projects/horowitz-agent/scripts/repair_audit_failures.py"}

covers **PDFs and scanned documents**.\n\n## Step 1: Remote URL Available?\n\nIf the document has a URL, **always try `web_extract` marker-pdf (~3-5GB) |\n|---------|-----------------|---------------------|\n| **Text-based PDF** | ✅ | ✅ |\n| **Scanned PDF (OCR)** | ❌ | ✅ (90+ languages) |\n| **Tables** | ✅ (basic) | ✅ (high Check available tooling:\n\n   ``​`bash\n   which tesseract || true\n   python - <<'PY'\n   for m in ['cv2','PIL','pytesseract','easyocr']:\n       try: __import__(m); print(m, 'yes')\n       except Exception as e: print(m, 'no', type(e).__name__)\n   PY\n   ``​`\n\n2. If Tesseract is available, preprocess crops with OpenCV/Pillow: rotate upright, upscale 2–3x, grayscale, denoise, threshold/contrast, then run Tesseract. Store OCR output separately from manual titles when possible (`ocrTitle`, `ocrText`, confidence/source), so the user can correct titles later without losing raw text.\n\n---\n\n## pymupdf (lightweight)\n\n``​`bash\npip install pymupdf pymupdf4llm\n``​`\n\nIf the runtime has `uv` but no `pip`/`ensurepip`, use an ephemeral dependency run instead of stopping:\n\n``​`bash\nuv run --with pymupdf python - If you have any objections to its publication, please contact us.\n\n---\n\n## Book Description\n\n### Core Theme\n\n*What You Do Is Who You Are* focuses on **how to intentionally create and shape business culture**.\n\nThe book argues that culture is not defined by slogans, stated values, marketing language, or internal messaging. Instead, culture is revealed by repeated behavior and action.\n\n### Important Excerpt\n\n> Who you are is not the values you list on the wall. It’s not what you say in company-wide meeting. It’s not your marketing campaign. It’s not even what you believe. Who you are is what you do.\n\n---\n\n## Main Ideas and Leadership Models\n\nBen Horowitz uses historical and modern examples to explain how leaders build culture through actions, rules, and lived behavior.\n\nThe book spotlights **four leadership and culture-building models**:\n\n1. **Toussaint Louverture**\n   - Leader of the only successful slave revolt.\n   - Associated with Haiti’s revolution.\n   - Used as a model for creating purposeful organizational culture.\n\n2. **The Samurai**\n   - Ruled Japan for approximately **700 years**.\n   - Helped shape modern Japanese culture.\n   - Presented as an example of disciplined cultural codes and behavioral norms.\n\n3. **Genghis Khan**\n   - Built the world’s largest empire.\n   - Used as an example of cultural inclusiveness and scalable organizational norms.\n\n4. **Shaka Senghor**\n   - Convicted of murder.\n   - Led a formidable prison gang.\n   - Eventually helped transform prison culture.\n\n---\n\n## Modern Case Studies Mentioned\n\nHorowitz connects historical leadership lessons to contemporary organizations and leaders, including:\n\n- **Reed Hastings at Netflix**\n  - Discussed in relation to cultural techniques resembling those of Toussaint Louverture.\n\n- **Travis Kalanick at Uber**\n  - Used as a modern case study for organizational culture and leadership implications.\n\n- **Hillary Clinton**\n  - Mentioned as another example in relation to cultural techniques and leadership behavior.\n\n- **Don Thompson**\n  - First African-American CEO of McDonald’s.\n  - Connected to Genghis Khan’s cultural inclusiveness.\n\n- **Maggie Wilderotter**\n  - CEO who led Frontier Communications.\n  - Also discussed in relation to inclusive and effective culture-building.\n\n---\n\n## Questions the Book Explores\n\nThe description frames the book as a journey through culture from ancient to modern times. It centers on the question:\n\n> who are we?\n\nIt also explores practical organizational identity questions:\n\n- How do people talk about us when we’re not around?\n- How do we treat our customers?\n- Are we there for people in a pinch?\n- Can we be trusted?\n\n---\n\n## Author Information\n\n### Ben Horowitz\n\nThe page describes **Ben Horowitz** as:\n\n- An American entrepreneur, investor, and author.\n- A leading IT entrepreneur.\n- Co-founder of **Andersen Horowitz**.\n- Born on **June 13, 1966**, in **London, England**.\n- Raised in **Berkeley, California, USA**.\n\nAdditional career detail from the page:\n\n> Horowitz co-founded the software company Obsauer, and served as CEO, until it was acquired by Hewlett-Packard for $1.6 billion in July 2007.\n\n*Note: The page appears to refer to “Obsauer,” likely intending Opsware.*\n\n---\n\n## Reader Engagement Features\n\nYSK Books includes gamified reading and engagement features:\n\n### Reading Rewards\n\n> Earn Rewards While Reading!\n\nReward rule:\n\n- Read **10 pages**\n- Spend **30 seconds on every page**\n- Earn **+5 points**\n\nThe page states:\n\n> Every 10 pages you read and spent 30 seconds on every page, earns you 5 reward points! {"content": "   130|Clearly, you can’t succeed if you don’t achieve both priority No. 1 and priority No. 2. So why is taking the market more important than not running out of cash? Because the only thing worse for an entrepreneur than start-up hell (bankruptcy) is start-up purgatory.\n   131|\n   132|What is start-up purgatory, you ask? Start-up purgatory occurs when you don’t go bankrupt, but you fail to build the No. 1 product in the space. You have enough money with your conservative burn rate to last for many years. You may even be cash-flow positive. However, you have zero chance of becoming a high-growth company. You have zero chance of being anything but a very small technology business (see Navisite). From the entrepreneur’s point of view, this can be worse than start-up hell since you are stuck with the small company.\n   133|\n   134|You recruited all the employees, you raised all the money and you made all the promises. You either see it through or leave–without your good reputation. No one wants to work for an entrepreneur who quits his or her own company. This is start-up purgatory, where you work just as hard, reap none of the rewards, and watch all your best people leave you. It sucks to be you.\n   135|\n   136|## The Bottom Line\n   137|\n   138|Spending a little or spending a lot is a means, not an end. Choose the right strategy to win the market or you may end up going straight to purgatory.\n   139|\n   140|As you listen to the virtues of the lean start-up–lightweight sales, light engineering, and so on–keep the following in mind:\n   141|\n   142|1. {"content": "    90|Complaints of type 2 are both more rare and more complex. If one of your executives summons the courage to complain about the competency of one of their peers, then there is a good chance that either the complainer or the targeted executive has a major problem. If you receive a type 2 complaint, you will generally have one of two reactions: a) they will be telling you something that you already know or b) they’ll be telling you shocking news.\n    91|\n    92|If they are telling you something that you already know, then the big news is that you have let the situation go too far. Whatever your reasons for attempting to rehabilitate the wayward executive, you have taken too long and now your organization has turned on the executive in question. You must resolve the situation quickly. Almost always, this means firing the executive. While I’ve seen executives improve their performance and skill sets, I’ve never seen one lose the support of the organization then regain it.\n    93|\n    94|On the other hand, if the complaint is new news, then you must immediately stop the conversation and make clear to the complaining executive that you in no way agree with their assessment. You do not want to cripple the other executive before you re-evaluate their performance. You do not want the complaint to become a self-fulfilling prophecy. Once you’ve shut down the conversation, you must quickly re-assess the employee in question. If you find that they are doing an excellent job, then you must figure out the complaining executive’s motivations and resolve them. Do not let an accusation of this magnitude fester. If you find that the employee is doing a poor job, there will be time to go back and get the complaining employee’s input, but you should be on a track to remove the poor performer at that point.\n    95|\n    96|## Closing Thought\n    97|\n    98|As CEO, you must consider the systemic incentives that result from your words and actions. While it may feel good in the moment to be open, responsive and action oriented, be careful not to encourage all the wrong things.\n    99|\n   100|is a cofounder and general partner at the venture capital firm Andreessen Horowitz.\n   101|\n   102|Ben Horowitz: The Fight Over Open Source AI Ben Horowitz, Theo Jaffee, and Sofia Puccini\n   103|\n   104|Travis is Back Ben Horowitz and Alex Danco\n   105|\n   106|Replay 2025: David Sacks on AI, Crypto, and America’s Technology Future Ben Horowitz, Marc Andreessen, David Sacks, and Erik Torenberg\n   107|\n   108|Adam Neumann: This Is How You Build Iconic Companies Adam Neumann, Marc Andreessen, Ben Horowitz, and Erik Torenberg\n   109|\n   110|Don’t Follow Your Passion | Ben Horowitz’s Advice for New Graduates Ben Horowitz\n   111|", "total_lines": 110, "file_size": 13731, "truncated": false, "is_binary": false, "is_image": false}

        "title": "Designing a Culture of Reinvention",
        "description": "# Designing a Culture of Reinvention\n## Transcript\n**Ben:** One of the reasons I really liked your book is you start from that honest place of “I screwed it up and now I need to figure it out.” You did that at your prior company Pure Software, which needed to reinvent itself, but couldn’t. What were the things that you ran into at Pure that kept you from being able to reinvent?\n\n**Reed:** I was a first-time manager. I had invented the first product Purify, which was a C debugging tool. I was an inexperienced CEO, and that manifested itself in several ways. One, in particular, was every time something went wrong, I didn’t want that to go wrong again, which is typically how an engineer approaches a problem.\n\nI viewed the organization as a big software puzzle, which I know is laughably simplistic, but that’s how I viewed it. And so, every time there was an error, we’d put a process in place to make sure that error didn’t happen. And, indeed, generally that particular error didn’t happen. But what I missed were the cultural effects of that year after year. And the cultural effect was that the people who prospered were the people who could develop and follow process well. That was the value system. If you followed the process well, you were rewarded in all kinds of ways. Over time it slowly drove out the creative mavericks who didn’t really want to deal with all that crap. The subtle thing is in the short term the business ran better, not worse, because it was very highly optimized. There was no negative feedback about it.\n\nThen the market shifted. In that case, it was C++ to Java, but the details don’t really matter. And we were unable to adapt. We ended up buying a bunch of companies to have new products for our sales force because we weren’t coming up with them ourselves. To do more acquisitions, it gets more complex because you’ve got this other company full of process, and eventually, we drowned.\n\nAnd so, I think of process as: it all feels good, but it builds up like barnacles on a boat. If you don’t, every now and then, scrape off the barnacles, then eventually a storm comes, and it sinks the boat. We always think about it a little like technical debt, another software metaphor. You’ve got to go in and scrape the barnacles and really try to get rid of process. Because I failed in that way in the past, I at least want to fail in a novel way this time.\n\n**Ben:** All new mistakes.\n\n**Reed:** That’s it. We’re pushing super hard into employee freedom, pushing into anti-process, as hard as we can.\n\n**Ben:** And to an amazing level. Why write this book now? You also had a co-author, which I thought was interesting. It wasn’t the normal co-author ghostwriter. It was almost like, “Well, what’s Reed saying? Yeah, that part is true. I was wondering about this part,” that kind of thing. Why that approach?\n\n**Reed:** Your books exempted, I have read way too many CEO pontification books, where the CEO says how great things are. I always wonder, “What’s the reality? What’s really happening in that company?” Now and then, I’ve got friends who work there, and I can get a beat on it, and it’s almost never what the CEO thinks.\n\nOf course, I knew that would be true at Netflix, too. So, I thought let me get someone really independent, who is a business school professor and has their own reputation, and give them open access to Netflix. They can interview anyone and everyone and report the reality to the reader.\n\nThe book is me pontificating, going through the theory, which I like doing, and then Erin saying, this is her observation of the reality from dealing with all the employees.\n\nShe had formerly written an incredible book “Culture Map,” which is about national cultures within a corporation. If you’re a global company, and you have a lot of Koreans and Germans and Brazilians and Dutch and English and Japanese, who are misunderstanding each other, then to read her book “Culture Map” is incredible. So, I knew she had a good insight about culture.\n\n**Ben:** Right. And did you end up learning things about Netflix. How did that process go with regards to actually running the company?\n\n**Reed:** As you would expect, if you have a good anthropologist spend a lot of time with 100 people, you pick up things – not that they are completely new, but their relative weighting – like the fact that we felt and feel like such an American company. I had thought, “Oh, we’re really doing well. I’m becoming pretty global.” And our Korean or our Dutch employees are like, “Wow, this is a super American company.” Worse, a Californian company.\n\nWe still have a way to go to get to the Holy Grail where everyone feels they have an equal chance to thrive whether you’re an employee for us in Mexico City or in Mumbai.\n\n### Optimizing for innovation (not efficiency)\n**Reed:** Now, we’re diving into feedback, but we should back up a little bit. For both you and I, we care about how you create long-term companies that continue to innovate.\n\nAnd the non-intuitive thing is it’s better to manage chaotically, if that’s productive and fertile.\n\nYou have to be very conscious as a leader how you’re optimizing for long-term innovation.\n\nThen there’s this creative sector, which we’re both in, and as managers we’ve inherited a cultural legacy that is highly optimized for manufacturing and safety with process, OKR

        "title": "What You Do Is Who You Are",
        "description": "# What You Do Is Who You Are\n### How to create your business culture\n#### By Ben Horowitz\n![](https://d1lamhf6l6yk6d.cloudfront.net/uploads/2023/04/wr26_Vector.svg)\n\nBen Horowitz, a leading venture capitalist, modern management expert, and New York Times bestselling author, combines lessons both from history and from modern organizational practice with practical and often surprising advice to help executives build cultures that can weather both good and bad times.\n\nBen Horowitz has long been fascinated by history, and particularly by how people behave differently than you’d expect. The time and circumstances in which they were raised often shapes them—yet a few leaders have managed to shape their times. In What You Do Is Who You Are, he turns his attention to a question crucial to every organization: how do you create and sustain the culture you want?\n\nTo Horowitz, culture is how a company makes decisions. It is the set of assumptions employees use to resolve everyday problems: should I stay at the Red Roof Inn, or the Four Seasons? Should we discuss the color of this product for five minutes or thirty hours? If culture is not purposeful, it will be an accident or a mistake.\n\nWhat You Do Is Who You Are explains how to make your culture purposeful by spotlighting four models of leadership and culture-building—the leader of the only successful slave revolt, Haiti’s Toussaint Louverture; the Samurai, who ruled Japan for seven hundred years and shaped modern Japanese culture; Genghis Khan, who built the world’s largest empire; and Shaka Senghor, a man convicted of murder who ran the most formidable prison gang in the yard and ultimately transformed prison culture.\n\nCulture is not like a mission statement; you can’t just set it up and have it last forever. There’s a saying in the military that if you see something below standard and do nothing, then you’ve set a new standard. This is also true of culture—if you see something off-culture and ignore it, you’ve created a new culture.\n\n**Ben Horowitz**\n\nWhat You Do Is Who You Are\n\nHorowitz connects these leadership examples to modern case-studies, including how Louverture’s cultural techniques were applied (or should have been) by Reed Hastings at Netflix, Travis Kalanick at Uber, and Hillary Clinton, and how Genghis Khan’s vision of cultural inclusiveness has parallels in the work of Don Thompson, the first African-American CEO of McDonalds, and of Maggie Wilderotter, the CEO who led Frontier Communications. Horowitz then offers guidance to help any company understand its own strategy and build a successful culture.\n\nWhat You Do Is Who You Are is a journey through culture, from ancient to modern. Along the way, it answers a question fundamental to any organization: who are we? How do people talk about us when we’re not around? How do we treat our customers? Are we there for people in a pinch? Can we be trusted?\n\nWho you are is not the values you list on the wall. It’s not what you say in company-wide meeting. It’s not your marketing campaign. It’s not even what you believe. Who you are is what you do. This book aims to help you do the things you need to become the kind of leader you want to be—and others want to follow.\n\n![](https://a16z.com/wp-content/themes/a16z/assets/images/wr25/quote-mark.svg)\n\nCulture is not like a mission statement; you can’t just set it up and have it last forever. There’s a saying in the military that if you see something below standard and do nothing, then you’ve set a new standard. This is also true of culture—if you see something off-culture and ignore it, you’ve created a new culture. ~ Ben HorowitzWhat You Do Is Who You Are",

      },
